# CPython #139834: JIT: Segfault in _Py_LazyJitTrampoline with ASan/UBSan enabled

## Metadata

- Issue: [JIT: Segfault in _Py_LazyJitTrampoline with ASan/UBSan enabled](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834)

## Fix Data

### PR #139271: gh-139269: Fix unaligned memory access in JIT code patching functions

- URL: [https://github.com/python/cpython/pull/139271](https://github.com/python/cpython/pull/139271)
- State: closed; merged: 2025-10-18T23:20:04Z; merge commit: [`bedaea059877`](https://github.com/python/cpython/commit/bedaea05987738c4c6b958d19cec9621bec09f07)
- Author: ashm-dev
- Changes: +20 / -11 across 2 file(s)

#### Description

```text
This PR fixes a series of misaligned memory access errors in the JIT engine that were causing segmentation faults when running tests with AddressSanitizer (ASan) and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (UBSan) enabled.

The unsafe direct pointer casts in `patch_32`, `patch_32r`, `patch_64`, and `patch_x86_64_32rx` have been replaced with `memcpy` to ensure safe, alignment-agnostic memory operations.

<!-- gh-issue-number: gh-139269 -->
* Issue: gh-139269
<!-- /gh-issue-number -->
```

**ashm-dev**, 2025-09-29T09:19:17Z:

```diff
@@ -393,7 +398,10 @@ patch_x86_64_32rx(unsigned char *location, uint64_t value)
 {
     uint8_t *loc8 = (uint8_t *)location;
     // Try to relax the GOT load into an immediate value:
-    uint64_t relaxed = *(uint64_t *)(value + 4) - 4;
+    uint64_t relaxed;
+    memcpy(&relaxed, (void *)(value + 4), sizeof(relaxed));
```

```text
No, should not cast to `uint64_t*`. Using `void*` is the correct and safer approach.

Casting to `uint64_t*` can cause undefined behavior due to:
1.  **Strict Aliasing Rule:** Accessing an object through a pointer of an incompatible type can lead to incorrect optimizations by the compiler.
2.  **Alignment Issues:** If the source address (`value + 4`) is not aligned for a `uint64_t`, creating a `uint64_t*` can cause a crash or performance degradation on some CPU architectures.

The `memcpy` function is designed to work with `void*` to safely copy raw bytes, avoiding these issues.
```

**picnixz**, 2025-09-26T18:15:21Z:

````text
> I believe it is like a type punning and should be opmizied

It depends on the optimization level. In `-O3`, alignment is essentially ignored and the assembly will look the same, whether you use memcpy or not, but that's with clang on `x86_64`. For ARM, the picture is different depending on the architecture. On ARMv7, the `memcpy` is expanded into

```asm
patch_32_memcpy:
        lsr     r1, r2, #24
        lsr     r3, r2, #16
        lsr     r12, r2, #8
        strb    r2, [r0]
        strb    r1, [r0, #3]
        strb    r3, [r0, #2]
        strb    r12, [r0, #1]
        bx      lr
```

On ARMv8, it's the same. I suggest playing with https://godbolt.org/z/zovMEW7Ta to see the differences.
````

**chris-eibl**, 2025-09-26T21:40:49Z:

````text
Interestingly, adding `-march=armv7` produces
```
patch_32_memcpy:
        str     r2, [r0]
        bx      lr
```
I would have assumed that's the default on `armv7-a clang`.

FWIW, `ARM GCC (unknown eabi)` behaves the same (producing the fast version only with `-march=armv7`), but `ARM GCC (linux)` produces the fast version since 9.3.0, before it needs `-march=armv7`, too.
````

```text
Yes, so far the description of the problem is in the form of a hypothesis, now I'm looking for options to fix it.

A preceding test in test_asyncio is corrupting some internal state (e.g., memory layout, an object's type, or internal JIT caches). This corruption does not cause an immediate crash but leads to a fatal error later when the JIT compiler attempts to process code during the "victim" test.
```

**ashm-dev**, 2025-09-29T16:26:54Z:

```text
@picnixz The JIT optimizer had a memory leak because it wasn't tracking dependencies on global and builtin namespaces when encountering `LOAD_GLOBAL` opcodes. This caused cached executors to become stale without proper invalidation when globals were modified. The fix adds explicit dependency tracking by registering `frame->f_globals` and `frame->f_builtins` in the dependencies bloom filter, resolving memory leaks in release builds. The issue still persists in debug builds and I'm currently working on fixing that.
```

**ashm-dev**, 2025-10-08T23:02:26Z:

````text
@picnixz 
You were right — the segfault is not related to my changes. Here’s how you can reproduce it in the main branch:

1. Build with the following script:

   ```bash
   #!/bin/bash
   set -e

   BUILD_DIR="build/debug"

   echo "--- Configuring and building in $BUILD_DIR ---"
   mkdir -p "$BUILD_DIR"
   cd "$BUILD_DIR"

   CC=clang CXX=clang++ ../../configure --with-pydebug \
       --with-address-sanitizer \
       --with-undefined-behavior-sanitizer \
       --enable-experimental-jit

   make -j$(nproc)

   echo "--- Build complete in $BUILD_DIR. Executable: $PWD/python ---"
   cd ../..
   ```

2. Run the tests with `python -m test`. They’ll likely fail with the same error I described in this [issue](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139269).

3. Start the REPL with `python`.

4. Type `import ins` and then press `Tab` for autocompletion.

5. Voilà — you’ll get a very unexpected segfault. It doesn’t only happen here, but this is the simplest case to reproduce. It seems to be related to `inspect`.


So maybe you could consider merging my current changes and reopening my [issue](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139288) related to this bug, since it appears to be the same underlying problem.
````

```text
I'm not sure if I bumped into this in https://github.com/python/cpython/pull/140310. But some UB is causing random fields in the frame to get overridden/read garbage values read out. Only when JIT is on. I'm inclined to merge to get rid of random UB in the JIT.
```

## Source Records

Complete verbatim content of the issue body and every comment. Sanitizer stacks, shadow bytes, allocation/free histories, and reproducers are preserved exactly as posted.

### Original Issue

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834)

````text
# Bug report

### Bug description:

When CPython is built on the `main` branch with the experimental JIT, AddressSanitizer (ASan), and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (UBSan) enabled, it produces a segmentation fault.

The crash is easily reproducible and appears to be triggered by operations that use the `inspect` module, such as Tab autocompletion in the REPL.

The full crash log shows that before the final `SEGV` in `_Py_LazyJitTrampoline`, UBSan reports several runtime errors related to **misaligned memory stores and loads** within `Python/jit.c`. This suggests that incorrect memory access is the root cause of the crash.

The final error reported by AddressSanitizer is:
`==...==ERROR: AddressSanitizer: SEGV on unknown address ... in _Py_LazyJitTrampoline`

This indicates a read from an invalid memory address, likely due to a corrupted pointer from the earlier misaligned access.

### **Steps to Reproduce**

1.  **Build CPython** from the `main` branch using the following configuration script:

    ```bash
    #!/bin/bash
    set -e
    
    BUILD_DIR="build/debug"
    
    echo "--- Configuring and building in $BUILD_DIR ---"
    mkdir -p "$BUILD_DIR"
    cd "$BUILD_DIR"
    
    CC=clang CXX=clang++ ../../configure --with-pydebug \
        --with-address-sanitizer \
        --with-undefined-behavior-sanitizer \
        --enable-experimental-jit
    
    make -j$(nproc)
    
    echo "--- Build complete in $BUILD_DIR. Executable: $PWD/python ---"
    cd ../..
    ```

2.  **Start the REPL** using the newly built executable:
    ```bash
    ./build/debug/python
    ```

3.  **Trigger the crash**: In the Python REPL, type `import ins` (without hitting Enter) and then press the **Tab key** for autocompletion.

4.  The REPL will immediately crash with a segmentation fault.

*(Note: Running the test suite with `python -m test` also fails with similar errors.)*

### **Full Crash Log (with UBSan and ASan)**

<summary>Direct interpreter crash (with clang 18)</summary>
````

### Comment 9 — picnixz, 2025-10-09T10:37:50Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385251066](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385251066)

```text
To summarize:

- Without #139271: we have misaligned reads/writes (UBs) and then ASAN failures.
- With #139271: the UB failures don't appear anymore in the traceback and only the ASAN remains. For me it's 50% of the time when I start the interpreter. I don't think it matters whether we use inspect or not here, it's just because we somehow use lazy trampolines.
```

### Comment 10 — ashm-dev, 2025-10-09T10:43:02Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385269199](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385269199)

```text
> I don't think it matters whether we use inspect or not here, it's just because we somehow use lazy trampolines.

Yeah, makes sense — I’ve also seen it outside of inspect, e.g. with unittest.mock, so it’s probably the same lazy trampoline issue.
```

### Comment 11 — ashm-dev, 2025-10-09T11:03:37Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385337643](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385337643)

````text
@picnixz You might try this one — it hits a segfault for me 100% of the time:
```bash
PYTHON_JIT=1 ./build/debug_fix/python <<'PY'
import unittest.mock as mock, selectors
def f():
    with mock.patch('selectors.SelectSelector'):
         pass
for _ in range(2000):
    f()
print("ok")
PY
```

````

### Comment 12 — picnixz, 2025-10-09T11:19:27Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385393090](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385393090)

```text
That's likely because we enable the trampolines for this snippet. IIRC, this can be verified by using `PYTHON_LLTRACE=3` and see that jitification happens. I don't think it'll help tracking down the issue more precisely though.
```

### Comment 14 — picnixz, 2025-10-09T11:31:25Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385434540](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3385434540)

```text
I don't think this specific trace will be useful (but I may be wrong) considering its size. I just wanted to tell that you can see whether the JIT is indeed used or not. Not all code will be JIT-ified; it depends on whether it's needed or not (and you can check if the JIT is effectively in effect by using PYTHON_LLTRACE). I suggest you try to do find a reproducer that has smaller JIT traces if possible.
```

### Comment 15 — ashm-dev, 2025-10-09T16:11:36Z

Source: [https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3386526700](https://github.com/python/cpython/issues/139834#issuecomment-3386526700)

````text
@picnixz 
You're right, the previous trace was too large. I've created a better, minimal reproducer that triggers the same segmentation fault.

This version uses a `while True` loop instead of a `for` loop. This avoids the overhead of the iterator opcodes (`FOR_ITER_RANGE`, etc.) and produces a much cleaner and shorter JIT trace, which should hopefully make debugging easier.

```python
def hot_loop():
    return True

if __name__ == "__main__":
    import os
    os.environ['PYTHON_JIT'] = "1"
    os.environ["PYTHON_LLTRACE"] = "3"

    while True:
        hot_loop()
```
````
